Sight senses this condition, because when it moves the whole visible thing, it finds the place facing its middle, whose image arises in the middle of sight, clearer than the remaining parts {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.320} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.321}.

## Chapter 4 (continued): the verification of the image by contemplation and movement, and the retention of images in the mind

It has been shown before that this meaning appears to sense when the visible thing is of wide extent. When sight perceives a visible thing and perceives its whole, it finds the image of the part facing its middle clearer than all the remaining parts. When it wishes to verify the image of the visible thing, it moves, and faces each part of the visible thing with its middle, and by this it perceives the image of each part of the visible thing clearly and verifiedly, as it perceived the part that faced its middle when it regarded the visible thing. So when the sense wishes to verify the image of a visible thing, sight moves until it faces each part of the visible thing with its middle, part after part, and by this movement it perceives the image of each part of the visible thing as clearly as it can be perceived. The discriminating faculty distinguishes everything that arrives at it in images: it distinguishes the parts and the difference of their colours when they differ in colour, the arrangement of the parts one among another and their articulation and the form of each one, all the meanings that appear from the visible thing by contemplation, and the form of the whole visible thing composed of those parts and those meanings. In this way each part of the visible thing is verified as it is, and all the meanings in the visible thing are verified. The image of each part of the visible thing does not arise verified, nor do all the meanings in the visible thing appear, except after sight has moved over all the parts, or the arrow, or what is near the arrow, has passed over each of those parts. Nevertheless sight is naturally disposed to the movement of contemplation and to passing the arrow of the ray over all the parts of the visible thing; when the discriminating faculty intends to contemplate the visible thing, it moves the arrow of the ray over all its parts. Since the subtle meanings in a visible thing appear only by the movement of sight and the passage of the arrow, or what is near the arrow, of the lines of the rays over every part of the visible thing, the image of the visible thing is not verified for the sense when its size is considerable, except by the movement of sight and the facing of each part of the visible thing by the middle of sight {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.321} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.322}.

Moreover, when a visible thing is extremely small and does not face the middle of sight, its contemplation is not completed until sight moves so that the arrow passes over that visible thing and the image of that visible thing arises in the middle of sight, and its image appears clearly. And sight does not perceive all the meanings in the visible thing except by discriminating all the meanings in all its parts. Since that is so, the contemplation by which the realities of the images of visible things are perceived takes place through sight itself, and through sight and discrimination together. Sight's perception of the reality of a visible thing's image takes place only by contemplation, and the contemplation by which the image of the visible thing is verified is completed only by the movement of sight; and when the size of the visible thing is considerable, its contemplation is completed only by the movement of the arrow of the ray, or what is near the arrow, of the lines of the rays over all the diameters of the visible thing. To this meaning went the one who held that seeing takes place only by movement, and that no visible thing is seen all at once; for he meant verified seeing, which takes place only by contemplation and by the movement of sight and the movement of the arrow of the ray over all the diameters of the visible thing {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.322}.

As for how the sense verifies the image of a visible thing by contemplation and movement: when sight faces a visible thing, and the image arises in sight, the sense perceives the whole image in general terms, and perceives the part at the tip of the arrow clearly, to the utmost that this part can be perceived, and at the same time perceives each of the remaining parts in the image somewhat. Then, when sight moves and the arrow passes from the part on which it was to another part, the sense perceives the whole image of the visible thing a second time, and perceives the part at the tip of the arrow a second time too; and its perception of this part at the second moment is clearer than at the first; and at this moment the sense also perceives the remaining parts somewhat. Likewise, when the arrow moves by motion to a third part, the sense perceives the whole visible thing a third time, and perceives the part at the tip of the arrow a third time, and its perception of this part at this moment is clearer than at the first two moments; and at the same time the sense perceives each of the remaining parts somewhat. So by sight's movement over the parts of the visible thing, the sense comes to two conditions: one in which its perception of the whole visible thing and of each of its parts is repeated, and the other in which it perceives each part of the visible thing by the arrow of the ray and what is near the arrow, as clearly as it can be perceived; so by this clarification all that can appear of those parts appears to the sense. When the sense's perception of the whole visible thing and of each of its parts has been repeated, and all that can appear of it has appeared, then in this condition it perceives all that can be perceived of that visible thing, but with repeated perception {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.322} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.323}.

Among the course of this whole and this repetition, the discriminating faculty distinguishes all that appears of the colours of the parts, their magnitudes, distances, shapes and positions, the equality of those that are equal in these meanings, and the difference of those that differ in all or some of these meanings, and the arrangement of the parts one among another. From the discrimination of all these meanings, and from comparing them with their like that it knows, it perceives the configuration composed of all of this for the whole visible thing. So by repetition, clarification and discrimination all the meanings in the visible thing are verified, and the configuration composed of all those meanings for the whole visible thing takes form in imagination. When all the meanings in the visible thing have been verified, and the configuration composed of all those meanings for the whole visible thing has taken form in imagination, the image of the visible thing is verified, by which that visible thing is particularised for the sense. In this way sight verifies the images of visible things by contemplation {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.323}.

Moreover, we say that when sight perceives a visible thing and its image is verified for the sense, the image of that visible thing remains in the mind and takes form in imagination; and when sight's perception of the visible thing has been repeated, its image is more firmly fixed in the mind than the image of a visible thing that sight has perceived only once, or has not perceived frequently. And when sight perceives one person and then perceives other persons of the same kind as that person, and its perception of persons of that kind is repeated and continues always, the image of that kind becomes fixed in the mind, and a universal image of that kind takes form in imagination in the mind {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.323}.

That images of visible things remain in the mind and in imagination is shown by this: when a person remembers someone he knows, whom he has seen before and been with, and whose image he has verified, and he remembers correctly the time at which he saw that person and the place in which he was with him, then at once he imagines the person's figure and the outline of his face and his form and his posture that he had at that time, and he imagines the place where he saw him, and he may at once imagine other visible things that were present in the place where he saw that person. His imagining of that person's image when he remembers him, and of the image of the place where he saw him, and of the condition in which he was, while that person is absent and that place is absent, is clear evidence that the image of that person and the image of that place are present in his mind and remain in his imagination. Likewise, when a person remembers a city he has seen and then left, he imagines the image of that city, and the images of the places where he was in that city, and the images of the people he knew in that city, if he remembers all of that correctly, though that city and what he saw in it are absent. Likewise, if a person remembers visible things he has seen and remembers correctly that he saw them, he imagines their images as he saw them at the time of seeing them. His imagining of the images of visible things he has seen before, while they are absent from him at the time of remembering them, is clear evidence that the images of visible things which sight has moved over arise in the mind and take form in imagination {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.323} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.324}.

## Chapter 4 (continued): the firmer retention of a repeated image, the universal images of kinds of visible thing, and the quiddity of visible things by likeness

As for why the image of a visible thing that sight perceives repeatedly is firmer in the mind and in imagination than the image of a visible thing that sight has not perceived repeatedly, the reason is this: when a meaning comes to the mind, its image arises in the mind. When time passes over that meaning and it does not return to the mind a second time, the mind may forget the meaning, or forget some of the meanings in it. When the meaning returns to the mind before it has been forgotten, and before the meanings in it, or most of them, have been forgotten, the image of that meaning is renewed in the mind; the mind then recalls the first image through the second, and the mind's recent acquaintance with that meaning is near to the second image. When the meaning comes to the mind many times, the mind is the more mindful of that meaning and more at home with it, and that meaning becomes firmer in the mind {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.324} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.325}.

Moreover, the first time a meaning comes to the mind, or the image of a visible thing comes to it, the mind may not have perceived all the meanings in that image and may not have verified them, and it may have perceived only some of the meanings in it. When the image returns a second time, the mind perceives in it what it did not perceive the first time. Each time the image recurs upon the mind, something appears in it that did not appear before, when not all the meanings in it had appeared the first time. When the mind has perceived from the image the subtle meanings of its details and all that is in it, and has verified its image, that image is clearer in the mind and firmer in imagination than an image in which the mind has not perceived all the meanings and has not verified its form. When the mind has perceived from the image all the meanings in it at the first time, and then the image has recurred upon it, and it has perceived no further meaning beyond the first time, it is verified that what it perceived at the first time is the reality of the image. The verified and certain image is firmer in the mind and in imagination than an unverified image. So when the image of a visible thing has been perceived repeatedly by sight, its form is verified for the mind and in imagination; the mind becomes more mindful of the image and more at home with it through the frequency of its recurrence, and the firmness of the image in the mind and in imagination comes from its verification and from the mind's recollection of it. For this reason an image that recurs upon sight is firmer in the mind and in imagination than an image of which sight has not perceived many instances {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.325}.

That meanings and images are firmer in the mind when they recur on it than meanings and images that do not recur on it is clearly shown by this: when a person wishes to memorise a branch of knowledge, or a piece of literature, or a report, or something like these, he repeats the reading of that meaning many times; when he repeats the reading, it becomes firm in his mind, and the more he repeats it, the firmer it becomes and the longer it is kept from being forgotten. If he reads it once, it does not become firm in his mind; if he reads it a few times, it does not become firm either, and if it does become firm, it is quickly forgotten. And when a person has forgotten something he had memorised, if he goes over it again and repeats it many times, the memory of that meaning returns and becomes firm in his mind. From consideration of this meaning it becomes clearly evident that the images that come to the mind, as they recur, become firmer in the mind and in imagination than images whose coming upon the mind has not recurred {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.325} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.326}.

As for the universal images that arise in the mind for kinds of visible thing and take form in imagination: each kind of visible thing has a configuration and a shape in which all the individuals of that kind are alike, while those individuals differ by particular attributes also perceived by the sense of sight; and sometimes the colour is one in all the individuals of the kind. The configuration, the shape, the colour, and all the meanings of which the configuration of each individual of the kind is composed, constitute a universal image of that kind. Sight perceives that configuration and that shape, and perceives every meaning in which the individuals of the kind are alike among all the individuals it perceives of that kind, and perceives also the particular attributes by which those individuals differ while agreeing in the universal meanings. So by the repeated perception of individuals of one kind, the universal image of that kind recurs upon sight, together with the differing particular images of those individuals. When the universal image recurs upon the mind it becomes fixed and settled in the mind; and from the difference of the particular images that come with the universal images as they recur, the mind perceives that the image in which all the individuals of that kind are alike is a universal image of that kind. In this way the universal images that sight perceives of kinds of visible thing arise in the mind and in imagination {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.326}.

So the images of individual visible things and the images of kinds of visible thing that sight has perceived remain in the mind and are fixed in imagination, and each time sight perceives them again its image is firmer in the mind and in imagination. Among the images that arise in the mind for kinds of visible thing and their individuals consists the sense's knowledge of visible things. The sense's reliance and dependence for perceiving the quiddities of visible things rests on the images that arise in the mind; for the perception of the quiddity of visible things takes place only by recognition, and recognition arises from measuring the image that sight perceives at the moment against the image fixed in imagination of the visible things that sight has perceived before, and from perceiving the likening of the image perceived at the moment to one of the images that arise in imagination. So the perception of the quiddity of a visible thing is the perception of its image's likeness to an image fixed in the mind and steady in imagination for kinds of visible thing. The sense's reliance in perceiving the quiddities of visible things rests on the universal images that arise in the mind for kinds of visible thing; and its reliance in knowing individual visible things rests on the images of those individuals that arise in the mind for each individual that sight has perceived before, and whose images it has imagined. The discriminating faculty is naturally disposed to liken the images of visible things, at the time of seeing, to the fixed image in imagination that the mind has acquired from images of visible things. When sight perceives a visible thing, the discriminating faculty seeks its likeness among the images that arise in imagination; if it finds in imagination an image resembling the image of that visible thing, it knows that visible thing and perceives its quiddity; if it does not find in imagination an image resembling the image of that visible thing, it does not know that visible thing and does not perceive its quiddity. Because the discriminating faculty likens the image of a visible thing at the time of seeing very quickly, it may sometimes err, and liken the visible thing to another visible thing when there is in the visible thing a meaning that is in that other; then, if it contemplates that visible thing afterwards and verifies its image, it likens it to its truly resembling image, and in the second condition it becomes clear to it that it was in error in the first likening. In these ways the quiddities of visible things are perceived by the sense of sight {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.326} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.327}.

## Chapter 4 (continued): perception by contemplation alone, and by contemplation with prior recognition, and signs

Since all these meanings have been shown, we say that the perception of visible things by contemplation takes two forms: perception by contemplation alone, and perception by contemplation with prior recognition. The perception by contemplation alone is the perception of strange visible things that sight has not distinguished before, and of visible things that sight has perceived before but whose seeing the viewer does not remember. When sight perceives a visible thing it has not seen before that time, and has not seen a thing of its kind, and the viewer wishes to verify its image, he contemplates it, and by contemplation surveys all the meanings in it, and perceives by contemplation its true image that is perceived by sight. If he has not seen that visible thing before that time, and has seen nothing of its kind, then at the moment of perceiving its image he does not know its image; he acquires by contemplation the image that is proper to it, and perceives its reality from its image, without having recognised it; so his verification of an image of this kind of visible thing is by contemplation alone. Likewise, when sight perceives a visible thing it has seen before, but the viewer does not remember having seen it, then on contemplating it, if he does not remember its earlier image, he does not know its image in the second condition; so his perception of a visible thing of this kind is by contemplation alone {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.327} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.328}.

As for the perception that takes place by contemplation with prior recognition, it is the perception of all kinds of visible thing that sight has perceived before, and has perceived visible things of their kind, and whose images of kind and of individuals have arisen in the mind, the mind remembering them and their images when it resumes contemplating them with its recognition. When sight perceives a visible thing that it perceived before that time and perceives something of its kind, then while regarding that visible thing it has perceived the whole of its image, which it perceives by immediacy; then by a little contemplation it perceives the whole of its configuration, which is the universal image proper to its kind. If it had already perceived visible things of that visible thing's kind, and the image of that kind has arisen in its mind, and it remembers the universal image of the kind of that visible thing, then it knows the universal image it perceives of that visible thing at the time of perceiving it; and in the state of knowing the universal image it perceives of that visible thing, it has known that visible thing by kind. Then, when it contemplates the remaining meanings in that visible thing, it verifies its particular image. If it has not seen that very visible thing before that time, or has seen it but does not remember having seen it or the image it perceived at the first seeing, it does not know the particular image, and when it does not know the particular image it does not know the visible thing itself; so its knowledge of that visible thing is by kind only, and from its contemplation and verification of the image it acquires the particular image that is proper to its individual. If it saw that visible thing before that time, along with seeing persons of its kind, and it remembers the seeing and the image it perceived of that visible thing earlier, then when it perceives the particular image it knows the particular image at the time of perceiving it, and in the state of knowing the particular image it has known the visible thing; so by the perception of its particular image it verifies the image of the visible thing, and at the same time knows the visible thing itself, and its knowledge of that visible thing is by kind and by individual together. If it has seen that visible thing before, and has seen of its kind only that one individual, and the universal image of that visible thing's kind has not been distinguished for it, then when it perceives that visible thing and perceives the universal meanings in it that are common to the kind of that visible thing, it does not know that visible thing nor perceive its quiddity from the perception of its universal image. When it perceives the remaining meanings in that visible thing, and perceives its particular image, and it remembers the particular image it perceived of that visible thing, then it knows the particular image when it perceives it, and when it knows the particular image it knows the very visible thing, and its knowledge of that visible thing is by the individual itself. No visible thing is perceived by contemplation except in one of these ways. So the perception of all visible things by contemplation takes two forms: perception by contemplation alone, and perception by contemplation with prior recognition; and recognition may be by kind only, or by kind and by individual together {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.328} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.329}.

Moreover, perception by contemplation takes place only in time, for contemplation is only by discrimination and the movement of sight, and discrimination takes place only in time and movement takes place only in time; so contemplation takes place only in time. It has also been shown before that perception by recognition and perception by discrimination take place only in time. Since it has been shown that the perception of visible things by contemplation takes two forms, by contemplation alone and by contemplation with prior recognition, and that what is perceived by contemplation and what is perceived by recognition are perceived only in time, we say that the perception that takes place by contemplation with prior recognition takes place, in most cases, in a shorter time than the time in which perception by contemplation alone takes place. For the meanings that are settled in the mind and present to memory do not need, at their presence, to survey all the meanings of which their reality is composed; it suffices for their perception to perceive one meaning that is proper to them. When the discriminating faculty perceives from the image that arrives in it a meaning proper to that image, and remembers the first image, it recognises by that property all the images that have arrived in it; for every meaning proper to an image is a sign showing that image {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.329}.

An example is a human figure: when sight perceives it, and perceives only the outline of a hand, it has perceived that it is a human being before perceiving the outline of the face, or the outline of its other parts; likewise if it perceives the outline of a leg, or the outline of the face before its other parts. From sight's perception of some of the meanings proper to the configuration of a human being, it has perceived that the visible thing is a human being without needing to perceive its other parts, because it perceives its other parts by prior recognition from the images arising in the mind of the configuration of a human being. Likewise, a particular individual that sight has seen before: when sight perceives some of the meanings proper to his particular image, such as a dent in his nose if he is a man, or blue in his eye, or a mark in his eyebrow, or wrinkles in his forehead, then from the perception of some of these signs together with its perception of the whole of his image, it has perceived and recognised that individual. Likewise a horse is recognised by a blaze, or part of a blaze, or a patch in some place on it, or a white mark on its forehead. Likewise in writing: a skilled scribe who perceives the form of the letter alif has perceived that it is alif from the whole of its form, and before examining its letters one by one he has perceived that it is alif; and likewise the scribe recognises all the words of the script that recur in a book, at the moment of perceiving them, from perceiving some of their letters, before examining each letter of them {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.329} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.330}.

## Chapter 4 (continued): perception by signs, and the difference in time between the kind and the individual

Visible things whose image sight has perceived before, while it knows their images and remembers them, may be perceived by sight through signs. It is not so with strange visible things that sight has not seen before, or visible things whose seeing sight has perceived and then forgotten. When sight perceives a visible thing it has not seen before that time, and perceives the outline of some of its parts, it does not perceive from that part the quiddity of that visible thing, because it has no settled image of its other parts; so sight does not perceive the reality of a visible thing it has not seen before except by surveying all its parts and all the meanings in it. Likewise a visible thing that sight has seen before, and whose seeing the viewer does not remember, is not verified in its image except after contemplating all the meanings in it. The perception of some of the meanings in an image takes place in a shorter time than the time in which all the meanings in the image are perceived. Sight's seeing that takes place by contemplation with prior recognition may, for the most part, take place in a shorter time than seeing by contemplation alone; and for this reason sight perceives familiar visible things very quickly, in a time hidden from sense, and in most cases there is no perceptible time between sight's facing a visible thing and its perception of the quiddity of that familiar visible thing {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.330} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.331}.

This is because from childhood and from the beginning of life a person perceives visible things, individuals of visible things recur upon him, and the universal images of kinds of visible thing recur upon him. It has been shown that the images of visible things that sight perceives arise in the mind and take form in imagination, and that images that recur upon sight are fixed in the mind and their form becomes settled in imagination. So all familiar visible things, all familiar kinds, and all well-known meanings have had their images settled in the mind, shaped in imagination, and present to memory. When sight perceives a familiar visible thing, and perceives its whole image, and then perceives a sign that is proper to that visible thing, it perceives the quiddity of that visible thing at the moment of perceiving that sign; and its perception of the visible thing is by prior recognition and by a little contemplation, to resume contemplating all the meanings in it at the moment of perceiving it that it has recognised. Familiar visible things are perceived by sight, and their quiddities are perceived, by signs and by prior recognition; so its perception of their quiddities, for the most part, takes place in a time that is not perceptible, because it takes place by a little contemplation and its perception of some of the meanings in them by contemplation {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.331}.

Moreover, sight's perception of the kind of a visible thing takes place in a shorter time than the time in which it perceives the individuality of the visible thing. For when sight perceives an individual person, it perceives a human being before it perceives his particular image that is proper to his individuality; it may perceive that he is a human being even before it perceives the outline of his face, perceiving from the uprightness of his stature and the arrangement of his limbs that he is a human being even though it has not distinguished his face. Likewise sight perceives the kind of familiar visible things by some signs proper to that kind. The perception of the individuality of a visible thing, however, is not like this, for the individuality of a visible thing is perceived only from the perception of the particular meanings proper to that individual, or the perception of some of them; and the perception of the particular meanings proper to the individual is not made except after the perception of the universal meanings in that individual, or the perception of some of them. In sum, the meanings in the universal image of the kind of an individual are some of the meanings in its individual image; and the perception of a part takes place in a shorter time than the perception of the whole; so sight's perception of the kind of a visible thing takes place in a shorter time than the perception of the individuality of that visible thing {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.331} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.332}.

Moreover, the times in which the kinds of familiar visible things are perceived differ, because some kinds of familiar visible thing resemble other kinds and some do not resemble others: the human kind, for example, does not resemble the kind of any other animal in its image; the horse is not so, for many beasts resemble the horse in the whole of its form. The time in which sight perceives from the figure of a human being his kind and perceives that he is a human being is not like the time in which sight perceives from the figure of a horse its kind and perceives that it is a horse, especially when the viewer perceives each of these from a considerable distance. For when sight perceives the figure of a human being moving, it at once perceives from the uprightness of his stature together with his motion that he is a human being: from the motion it perceives that he is an animal, and from the uprightness of the stature it perceives that he is a human being. It is not so when sight perceives the figure of a horse; for when sight perceives the figure of a horse, and perceives it moving, and perceives together with the whole of that its form and the number of its legs, it does not perceive from all of this that it is a horse; for these meanings are in many four-footed animals, and they are equal to the horse in these and other meanings, especially the mule, which resembles the horse in most of its conditions. The horse is distinguished from the mule only by meanings that are not all apparent, such as the markings of its face, the length of its neck, the speed of its motion, and the breadth of its stride. If sight does not perceive one of these meanings by which the horse is distinguished, along with its perception of the whole of its form, it does not perceive that it is a horse. The time in which sight perceives the uprightness of a human being's stature is not like the time in which it perceives the form of a horse together with the particular meanings by which the horse is distinguished from other creatures. Sight's perception of the kind of a human being takes place in a shorter time than its perception of the kind of a horse, although both times are short, and one of them, according to the changing conditions, exceeds the other {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.332} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.333}.

Likewise, when sight perceives the rosy flush of a rose in some garden, it at once perceives that the thing seen is a rose, by the colour that belongs to the rose even while it stands in the garden, before it perceives the roundness of its petals, the overlapping of the petals one upon another, or all the meanings from which the image of the rose is composed. Although the rose may resemble other flowers, sight perceives in every case that it is a bloom and not a leaf of a tree or of a plant. It is not so when sight perceives the greenness of basil in a garden: greenness alone does not show that it is basil, since most plants are green and many resemble basil in greenness and shape, such as the nammam and similar plants. If sight does not perceive the form of the basil leaves, their closeness of growth, and the meaning that marks basil out, it does not perceive that it is basil. The time in which sight perceives the shapes of basil leaves and the meanings that particularise basil, along with its greenness, is not the time in which it perceives only the rosy flush of a rose. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.333}

Familiar visible things are all perceived in their kinds and their individuals with little contemplation once knowledge has advanced, and in most cases their perception takes place in a time that is not sensed. The times of their perception differ according to the difference of their kinds and of their individuals. The perception of the kind of a person is quicker than the perception of his individuality, the perception of the kind of a thing whose likeness is rare is quicker than the perception of the kind of a thing whose likeness is common, and the perception of the individuality of a person of rare likeness is quicker than the perception of the individuality of a person of common likeness. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.334}

Moreover, the time of contemplation differs according to the meanings contemplated in visible things. When sight perceives an animal with many legs, whose legs are small and which is moving, it perceives its motion with little contemplation; once it perceives the motion, it perceives that the thing is an animal, and then, with little contemplation of its legs, it perceives that it has many legs from the separation between them. Yet it does not know at once how many legs it has. If it wishes to know their number, it needs further contemplation and further time, for the number is not perceived until sight fixes upon each leg and counts it, and this takes a time that has a measure, which varies with the number of the legs. Likewise, when sight perceives a round figure within which is a many-sided figure, with very small and closely varying unequal sides that are not markedly unequal, it perceives in the first moment of perceiving the whole figure that it is round, and does not perceive at once that within it there is a polygon. If it contemplates the round figure a little longer, the polygon within it appears. So its perception of the roundness of the figure is quicker than its perception of the polygon within. Then, at the moment of perceiving the polygon, the difference of its sides does not appear, nor does it distinguish whether they are equal or unequal, when the sides are small and their difference is slight and not marked, except by further contemplation and in a time that has a measure. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.334} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.335}

Moreover, when the sense wishes to contemplate the shape of a visible thing as a whole, it is enough for sight to pass over its outline only. Likewise, to contemplate its colour it is enough for sight to pass over it in a sweep, and to contemplate the roughness or smoothness of its surface, or its transparency or density, a quick sweep is enough. It is not so with the hidden and subtle meanings in visible things, such as the shapes of each of their parts and the resemblance of those shapes, the sizes of the parts and their differences, the differences and resemblances of their colours, and the arrangement of the small parts one beside another, where the visible thing has small distinct parts. These meanings are not perceived by contemplation until sight fixes upon each part, circles round the shape of each part, and measures each part against the others; this does not complete itself in a short time and by a quick movement, but in a time that has a measure, and so with all subtle meanings. Thus the times of sight's contemplation differ according to the difference of the meanings contemplated. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.335}

Having made all this clear, we say that the seeing which takes place through prior knowledge, when it is by signs and little contemplation, and sight does not resume contemplating all the meanings in the visible thing, is not a verified perception. For the perception of a visible thing through prior knowledge and through signs perceives the whole of the visible thing as it is, and the discerning faculty perceives the particular meanings in that visible thing in the manner that knowledge already holds of that visible thing, from the first form that is present in the soul. The particular meanings in visible things may change with the passing of time, yet sight does not perceive the changes in a visible thing by means of prior knowledge. If the change is hidden and not wholly apparent, sight does not perceive it at once, and a change that is not very evident is not perceived except by contemplation. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.335} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.336}

For example, if sight knows a certain person whose appearance was sound, and then that person was absent from sight for a time, and during his absence a blemish, marks or a freckle appeared on his face, which were hidden and not wholly apparent, and sight then perceived him afterwards and recognised him, then at the moment of perceiving and recognising him it does not perceive the freckle or the marks that appeared on his face when they are not fully apparent, and it believes him to be sound in form because of what it knew of his form before. If it does not resume its contemplation, it has perceived that visible thing otherwise than it is; if it contemplates him a little longer, the marks on his face appear to it and it perceives his form as it is. Likewise, if sight perceives a fruit and contemplates it and verifies its form, then leaves it for days, during which the fruit grows, its size increases and its shape changes, or part of it has a redness that grows or deepens, and the change is not sharp but slight, then when sight returns to the fruit and sees and recognises it, at the moment of perceiving it and recognising it, it does not perceive the slight change that has happened to it. If it resumes contemplating it in the second state, while still keeping in mind the true form it had in the first state, it perceives the change that has taken place and verifies its form in the second state. If it does not resume its contemplation, the form it perceived of that fruit through prior knowledge is not its true form as it is at the moment of perceiving it in the second case. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.336}

Likewise, when sight perceives a wall in some place, smooth and with carvings and ornaments, examines it and verifies its form, then leaves that place for a time, and during its absence the wall changes in the roughness of its surface, or some of its carvings wear away, not wholly apparently, then when sight returns to the place and sees the wall, remembering its first form, at the moment of seeing and recognising it, it does not perceive the hidden wear that has taken place, and it knows the wall's form as one without that wear. If roughness has come to it, it takes the wall to be smooth as it was known; if its carvings were clear at first and then changed, it believes the carvings in the second state to be clear. So at the moment of perceiving and recognising the wall, it perceives its form by prior knowledge. If it does not resume contemplation, it has perceived the form otherwise than it is; if it resumes contemplation, the meanings that have changed in the wall appear to it and it perceives the form as it is. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.336} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.337}

All visible things in the world of generation and corruption are open to change in their colours, shapes, sizes, conditions, smoothness, roughness, the arrangement of their parts, and many of the particular meanings in them, because their nature is changeable and they are disposed to be affected by what befalls them from outside. Change is natural to them, and a change that sight can perceive is possible in all of them, even though some changes in their substance cannot appear to sight. No visible thing in the world of generation and corruption is incapable of receiving a change that appears to sight. Since all visible things are disposed to change and can change in a way apparent to sight, no visible thing that sight has perceived before, verified, and remembers can be trusted, when it sees it again, to be still in the form it had at first, since change is possible in all of them. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.337} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.338}

Perception of visible things through prior knowledge, through signs, and through little contemplation is therefore not a verified perception. Sight perceives a visible thing in a verified way only by contemplating it while it perceives it, and by surveying all the meanings in it and distinguishing all of them at the moment of perceiving it. Seeing takes two forms: seeing by immediacy and seeing by contemplation. Seeing by immediacy perceives only the apparent meanings, and the form of the visible thing is not verified by immediacy. Immediacy alone is the seeing of visible things that sight does not know and does not attend to at the moment; immediacy with prior knowledge is the seeing of visible things whose kind sight knew before, which it recognises when it attends to them without resuming contemplation. In both cases sight does not perceive the reality of the visible thing by immediacy, whether or not it knew it before. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.338}

Seeing by contemplation takes two forms: contemplation alone, and contemplation with prior knowledge. Contemplation alone is the seeing of visible things that sight has not perceived before, or whose earlier perception it does not recall when it contemplates them at the moment of perceiving them. Contemplation with prior knowledge is the seeing of all visible things that sight has perceived before and remembers seeing, when it resumes contemplating them with their recognition and surveys the meanings in them. This seeing divides into two. The first is the familiar seeing of familiar visible things, which takes place by signs perceived with little contemplation and by surveying some of the meanings of the visible thing with prior knowledge; it usually occurs in a time that is not sensed, and what is perceived in this way is not perceived with the utmost verification. The second is seeing through utmost contemplation, surveying all the meanings in the visible thing while perceiving it with prior knowledge of it; it usually occurs in a sensed time, which varies with the meanings in the visible thing. It is by this seeing that familiar visible things are perceived with the utmost verification. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.338} {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.339}

In sum, sight perceives nothing of the visible things with complete verification except by contemplating all the meanings in the visible thing, examining all its parts, and distinguishing all its meanings while sight perceives it, whether or not sight knew it before. This verification is relative to the sense, and by verified and utmost verified in these passages is meant the utmost that the sense perceives. Yet perception of visible things takes place according to the strength of sight, since seeing differs in its sensing in strength and weakness. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.339}

Thus perception of visible things takes place in these ways. These are all the kinds of seeing, which is what this chapter set out to explain. We have set out in detail all visible things and all visible meanings, shown every meaning by which sight arrives at perceiving visible things and visible meanings, and distinguished all the divisions into which the kinds of seeing divide. These are the meanings this treatise aimed to make clear. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.339}

The second treatise of the Book of Optics by al-Hasan ibn al-Hasan is complete. The copying ended on the evening of Sunday, the twenty-eighth of Jumada al-Akhira in the year 476 H, at Basra, and the copy was written by Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Ja'far. This colophon is the copyist's record, as read in the scan, and is not a date fixed by the edition. {cite:manazir-sabra-1983 scan-p.339}
